Post-processed frozen-flow methods for the long time sampling of ergodic dynamics on Riemannian manifolds
Adrien Busnot Laurent, Sébastien Macé

Abstract
In this work, we propose a novel intrinsic approach to the approximation of ergodic SDEs on Riemannian manifolds, which include Riemannian Langevin dynamics. In opposition to the standard extrinsic approaches such as penalization methods and projection methods, our methodology does not use embeddings or coordinates and only relies on natural geometric operations: geodesics, parallel transport,…We give a criterion for high order of accuracy for the invariant measure, develop new intrinsic numerical methods designed solely for sampling the invariant measure, and derive high order conditions using a new algebraic operation on exotic Lie-Butcher series. In the spirit of the Leimkuhler-Matthews method, our approach prioritizes long time sampling efficiency over finite time accuracy, and outperforms the previous extrinsic and intrinsic approaches in terms of cost for a given accuracy, which we illustrate with several numerical experiments. Keywords: geometric numerical integration, stochastic differential equations, Riemannian manifolds, Riemannian Langevin, ergodicity, Lie-group methods, frozen-flow, Butcher series, exotic series, post-Lie algebra, Hopf algebra, order conditions. AMS subject classification (2020): 16T05, 41A58, 60H35, 37M25, 65L06, 70H45.

1
footnotetext:
Univ Rennes, INRIA (Research team MINGuS), IRMAR (CNRS UMR 6625) and ENS Rennes, France.
Adrien.Busnot-Laurent@inria.fr,
Sebastien.Mace@univ-rennes.fr.

1
Introduction

The aim of this paper is the design of high-order sampling methods of ergodic stochastic dynamics on Riemannian manifolds.
More precisely, let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a smooth, complete, connected
D
-dimensional Riemannian manifold endowed with a metric
g
and let
\nabla
be its associated Levi-Civita connection. Let
E_{1},\dots,E_{D}
be a global orthonormal frame basis (for simplicity), that is, for all
x
in
\mathcal{M}
, the set
\{E_{1}(x),\dots,E_{D}(x)\}
is an orthonormal basis of the tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
.
Given a smooth and Lipschitz vector field
F:x\mapsto\underset{d=1}{\overset{D}{\sum}}f^{d}(x)E_{d}(x)
, we consider stochastic differential equations on
\mathcal{M}
of the following form:

\mathrm{d}X(t)=F(X(t))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\sum_{d=1}^{D}E_{d}(X(t))\circ\mathrm{d}W_{d}(t),\hskip 10.00002ptX(0)=X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}.

(1.1)

Equation (
1.1
) can be understood as a SDE with additive noise, as it rewrites (see
[
33
]
) as

\mathrm{d}X(t)=(F+\nabla_{E_{n}}E_{n})(X(t))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}B_{\mathcal{M}}(t),

where
B_{\mathcal{M}}(t)
is a Brownian motion on
\mathcal{M}
and we use the Einstein summation notation.
The class of SDEs (
1.1
) includes the celebrated Riemannian Langevin dynamics

\mathrm{d}X(t)=-\nabla V(X(t))\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}B_{\mathcal{M}}(t),

(1.2)

when
F
derives from a potential, that is, when for a smooth function
V\colon\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, one has

F=-\nabla V-\nabla_{E_{n}}E_{n},\hskip 10.00002ptf^{d}=-E_{d}[V]-\left\langle\nabla_{E_{n}}E_{n}|E_{d}\right\rangle.

(1.3)

Under growth assumptions on
F
(see, for instance, the Bakry-Emery criterion
[
7
]
for the Langevin case (
1.2
)), the flow
X(t)
of (
1.1
) is ergodic, that is, its long-time behaviour is described by a deterministic measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=\rho_{\infty}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
, that is absolutely continuous with respect to the Riemannian volume form
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
, in the sense that for a large class of smooth functions
\varphi
and for all initial condition
X_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
, one has

\underset{T\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varphi(X(t))\mathrm{d}t=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)\text{ almost surely.}

In the case of Riemannian Langevin dynamics (
1.2
), the invariant measure is given by the Gibbs density
\rho_{\infty}\propto e^{-V}
.

In this paper, we design new numerical methods that have a high order of accuracy for sampling the invariant measure of (
1.1
).
We consider one-step numerical integrators
{(X_{n})}_{n=0,\dots,N}
, on a discretization
t_{n}=nh
of a time interval
[0,T]
for a time step
h=T/N
, given by a random perturbation of the identity:

X_{n+1}=\Psi_{h}(X_{n}),\hskip 10.00002ptX_{0}\in\mathcal{M}.

(1.4)

We also consider post-processed methods

X_{0}\in\mathcal{M},\hskip 10.00002ptX_{n+1}=\Psi_{h}(X_{n}),\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{X}_{N}=\overline{\Psi}_{h}(X_{N}),

(1.5)

where the post-processor
X\mapsto\overline{X}
is a random perturbation of the identity map on
\mathcal{M}
, that is applied only once at the very end of the trajectory.
Assuming ergodicity of the numerical method (
1.4
) for a measure
\mathrm{d}\mu^{h}_{\infty}=\rho^{h}_{\infty}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
,
we propose methods of high order of accuracy
p>1
for the invariant measure of (
1.1
), that is, that satisfy for
h
small enough,

\left|\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\mathrm{d}\mu^{h}_{\infty}-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}\right|\leq Ch^{p}.

A crucial difficulty of the approach is that the integrators have to evolve on
\mathcal{M}
in order for the measure
\mathrm{d}\mu^{h}_{\infty}
to be absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
.
Moreover, our approach focuses on
intrinsic
integrators, that are, numerical methods that evolve on
\mathcal{M}
and that do not depend on an embedding of
\mathcal{M}
in a higher-dimensional Euclidean space or on a choice of coordinates.
Among the existing
extrinsic
approaches, we mention the popular projection methods (see
[
46
,
40
]
and references therein), which are conveniently implemented by embedding
\mathcal{M}
in a vector space of higher dimension, but often face severe timestep restrictions.
We also mention the existing intrinsic approaches for SDEs on manifolds
[
51
,
53
,
9
,
49
]
, that either focus on order one of accuracy or strong convergence, or are defined on specific manifolds only (i.e., Lie groups).
Our approach relies on the class of frozen-flow methods recently introduced in
[
14
]
, on which we add post-processors
[
65
]
. This class of methods can be seen as a stochastic extension of the Crouch-Grossman and commutator-free Lie group methods
[
23
,
62
,
34
,
22
,
61
]
.
In opposition to
[
51
,
53
]
, our approach generalises the Lie group methods to any smooth manifold and focuses only on the approximation for the invariant measure.
An important feature of the new integrators is that they do not rely on the use of embeddings or on local coordinates. Their formulation, convergence analysis, order theory, and implementation are entirely intrinsic.

To derive methods of high order for the invariant measure, it is sufficient to use methods of weak order
p
as they will have at least order
p
for the invariant measure
[
64
]
. However, there exists many efficient methods of low weak order and high order for the invariant measure in a variety of (Euclidean) contexts
[
11
,
42
,
3
,
43
,
15
]
.
Among these, the celebrated Leimkuhler-Matthews method
[
42
]
is of weak order one and second order for the invariant measure for solving (
1.1
) when
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{D}
:

\displaystyle H_{n}=X_{n}+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d},\hskip 10.00002ptX_{n+1}=X_{n}+hF(H_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d},\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{X_{N}}=X_{N}+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\overline{\xi}^{d}_{n}E_{d}.

Our intrinsic approach with frozen-flow methods allows us to derive in particular a straightforward generalisation of the Leimkuhler-Matthews method to the Riemannian setting,

where only one evaluation of the vector field
F
is needed (see Section
2.3
for the details).
We recall that, in comparison, the extrinsic approach with projection methods from
[
40
]
requires four evaluations of
F
, has a technical implementation, and faces stability issues.

The derivation of the order conditions for the invariant measure relies on exotic Lie-Butcher series and on the Weitzenböck connection algebra.
The exotic Lie-Butcher series were introduced in
[
14
]
for deriving weak high-order estimates. They generalise naturally the exotic Butcher series
[
39
,
35
,
12
,
13
]
and the Lie-Butcher series
[
34
,
61
,
58
]
.
While such formalism is necessary for dealing with the intricate calculations of order conditions, we emphasize that the algebraic objects considered here are interesting beyond their numerical use.
We mention in particular the works
[
13
,
38
,
14
]
that study the geometric and algebraic properties of exotic forests and the works
[
57
,
27
,
4
,
30
,
55
]
that draw strong links between planar trees and computations in specific connection algebras.
The numerical integrators considered here rely on elementary geometric operations (geodesic, parallel transport) for a specific curvature-free connection, called the Weitzenböck connection. This choice allows us to use planar trees for the calculations, to which we add the exotic feature for representing the Laplace-Beltrami operator in terms of forests.
Thanks to this algebraic formalisation, we provide a methodology for the design of intrinsic integrators of any high-order for the invariant measure.
More precisely, we define the integration by parts of planar exotic forests, thus generalising
[
39
,
12
,
13
]
to the manifold setting, and we describe the order conditions for the invariant measure by a character on a modified shuffle algebra of exotic forests.
Contrary to the Euclidean case where such theory naturally leads to modified equations of any order and stochastic backward error analysis for the invariant measure, there are profound algebraic differences on manifolds that make intrinsic stochastic backward error analysis challenging.

The paper is organised the following way.
We present in Section
2
the main results of the paper. After presenting the notation and main assumptions, we give a convenient characterisation of the high-order for the invariant measure, generalising the Euclidean works
[
24
,
2
,
65
]
. Then, we apply this criterion to derive new simple methods of second order for the invariant measure.
The proof of the criterion for high order for the invariant measure is presented in Section
3
.
In Section
4
, we apply the exotic Lie-Butcher series formalism for the derivation of order conditions for the invariant measure, and we extend the integration by parts on exotic forests to the planar context.
We present numerical experiments on a variety of classical manifolds in Section
5
and we present outlooks and future works in Section
6
.

2
Preliminaries, characterization of the invariant measure and new numerical methods

2.1
Notation and main assumptions

For
v\in T\mathcal{M}
, let
\left|v\right|=\sqrt{g(v,v)}
be its Riemannian norm. For

o\in\mathcal{M}
. Then we denote by
r=d(o,\cdot)
the Riemannian distance
map. It is 1-Lipschitz and
r^{2}
is smooth on
\mathcal{M}\setminus Cut_{o}
.
For all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}
,
\mathrm{d}\varphi
is its differential and

\left|\mathrm{d}\varphi\right|
its Riemannian norm. We denote by
v[\varphi](x)
the differential
\mathrm{d}\varphi(x)\cdot v
of

\varphi
in the direction of
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}
at the point
x\in\mathcal{M}
. Let
denote the set of test function by
C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, the set of smooth functions
whose derivatives of all orders have polynomial growth and satisfy estimates of
the form

\left|E_{d_{q}}[\dots E_{d_{1}}[\varphi]\dots]\right|(x)\leq C(1+{r(x)}^{K}),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ for }q=0,1,\dots

Let
F:x\mapsto\underset{d=1}{\overset{D}{\sum}}f^{d}(x)E_{d}(x)
be a
vector field and its unique decomposition in the orthonormal frame, then
F

is an element of
\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
if
f^{d}\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
and if its components are Lipschitz, that is,
\left|\mathrm{d}f^{d}\right|\leq C
.
Equipped with the Jacobi bracket
{[-,-]}_{J}
, the space
(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M}),{[-,-]}_{J})
is a Lie algebra.

Definition 2.1
.

The Weitzenböck affine connection
\vartriangleright:\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
, is given by

(X\vartriangleright Y)=(X[Y^{d}])E_{d}.

We extend this notation to functions by
X\vartriangleright\varphi=X[\varphi]
.

Remark 2.2
.

The choice of the Weitzenböck connection was already implicitly made in
[
61
]
. It can be shown that its associated curvature vanishes. If one further assumes that the torsion
T
is constant, then
(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M}),\vartriangleright,-T)
is a post-Lie algebra
[
30
]
.

In the spirit of
[
50
,
30
,
14
]
, let the differential operators on
\mathcal{M}
be given by the tensor algebra
T(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M}))
whose product is denoted by
\cdot
.
Differential operators act on functions
\varphi
by:

(X_{1}\cdots X_{n})\vartriangleright\varphi=x^{i_{1}}_{1}\cdots x^{i_{n}}_{n}E_{i_{1}}[\ldots E_{i_{n}}[\varphi]\ldots],

where
X_{j}=\sum_{i_{j}=1}^{D}x^{i_{j}}_{j}E_{i_{j}}
is the unique decomposition of the vector field
X_{j}
in the frame basis.

Remark 2.3
.

The Laplace-Beltrami operator is expressed by

\Delta\varphi=\underset{d=1}{\overset{D}{\sum}}\left(E_{d}\cdot E_{d}-\nabla_{E_{d}}E_{d}\right)\vartriangleright\varphi.

The product
\vartriangleright
extends to
T(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M}))
by the so-called Guin-Oudom process
[
60
,
28
]
, which extends straightforwardly in a curvature-free setting (see
[
14
]
).
The space of differential operators
(T(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})),\cdot,\Delta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup})
is a Hopf algebra when equipped with the deshuffle coproduct
[
50
,
30
]
. In the context of a connection with constant torsion,
(T(\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})),\cdot,\Delta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup},\vartriangleright)
yields a post-Hopf algebra
[
48
]
. Note that taking into account the action of differential operators on functions would naturally yield post-Hopf algebroid structures
[
14
,
18
]
.

The generator
\mathcal{L}
of the SDE (
1.1
) is defined by

\mathcal{L}\varphi=\underset{d=1}{\overset{D}{\sum}}(f^{d}E_{d}+(E_{d}\cdot E_{d}))\vartriangleright\varphi.

(2.1)

In the case of the Langevin equation (
1.2
), the generator is expressed as

\mathcal{L}\varphi=-\nabla V[\varphi]+\Delta\varphi.

(2.2)

To ensure that the equation (
1.1
) is well-posed and that the
solution does not blow up, we shall assume the following.

Assumption 2.4
.

The vector fields
E_{1},\dots,E_{D}
are smooth and bounded.
The vector field
F
belongs to
\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
.
The generator satisfies

\mathcal{L}r^{2}\leq\nu+\lambda r^{2}

(2.3)

on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathrm{Cut}_{o}
for some constants
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\nu\geq 1
.

There exist various criteria which ensure that
inequality (
2.3
) is satisfied. The first criterion is
compactness, which can be used for classical manifolds such as the sphere

\mathds{S}^{n}
and the Lie group
SO_{q}(\mathds{R})
. This condition being
seldom satisfied, a second classical criterion is the Bakry-Émery criterion
from
[
7
]
. This criterion applies to the case of
equation (
1.1
), where the generator writes as (
2.2
). One says that the potential
V
satisfies
the criterion if there exists
\kappa\in\mathds{R}
such that

\Ric+\Hess(V)\geq\kappa.

The term
\Ric
designs the Ricci tensor on
(\mathcal{M},g)
and is
defined as the trace of the Riemann tensor. Note that this handy criterion has
been adapted in
[
6
]
for equation (
1.1
) with
a lower bound of the operator
\Ric-\nabla F
. The moment conditions
from
[
47
]
implies stochastic completeness.

Under Assumption
2.4
, the equation (
1.1
)
generates a Markovian semigroup on
C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. For all
\varphi\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, the function
u:(t,x)\mapsto\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X(t))|X(0)=x\right]
satisfies the Kolmogorov equation

\partial_{t}u=\mathcal{L}(u),\hskip 10.00002ptu(0,x)=\varphi(x).

(2.4)

To the contrary to
[
14
]
, we are interested in the
behaviour in long time of the solution of (
1.1
).

Definition 2.5
.

A process
X
is ergodic if there exists
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}
a unique invariant measure with density function
\rho_{\infty}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
, such that for all
\varphi\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
and for all initial condition
X(0)\in\mathcal{M}
, it follows that

\underset{T\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{T}\underset{0}{\overset{T}{\int}}\varphi(X(s))\mathrm{d}s=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)\text{ almost surely,}

(2.5)

where
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
is the Riemannian measure on
\mathcal{M}
defined in
[
41
]
.

Definition 2.6
.

A numerical method (
1.4
) is ergodic if there exists
\mathrm{d}\mu^{h}
a unique invariant measure with density function
\rho^{h}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
, such that for all
\varphi\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
and for all initial condition
X(0)\in\mathcal{M}
, it follows that

\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{N+1}\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\varphi(X_{n})=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\text{ almost surely.}

(2.6)

The method is of order
p\geq 1
for the invariant measure if there exists
h_{0}>0
and
C>0
which depends on
h_{0}
and
\varphi
such that for all
h\in(0,h_{0})
,

\left|e(\varphi,h)\right|\leq Ch^{p}\text{ with }e(\varphi,h)=\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{N+1}\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\varphi(X_{n})-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y).

(2.7)

In the spirit of
[
2
]
, the ergodicity of the stochastic process is a prerequisite of our analysis.

Assumption 2.7
.

Under Assumption
2.4
, there exists a unique invariant measure
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}
with density
\rho_{\infty}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
is the unique solution of the equation

\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0,

(2.8)

with
\mathcal{L}^{*}
the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
.

In the case of the Riemannian Langevin equation (
1.2
),
Assumption
2.7
is automatically satisfied and the density

\rho_{\infty}
is explicitly given by the Gibbs measure:

\rho_{\infty}=e^{-V}/Z,\hskip 10.00002ptZ=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}e^{-V}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}.

(2.9)

Assumption 2.8
.

For all function
g\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
with zero mean on
\mathcal{M}
, there exists a unique function
\mu\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\mathcal{L}^{*}\mu=g
and
\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\mu(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)=0
.

The following assumption from
[
14
]
, automatically satisfied on

\mathds{R}^{d}
, ensures the regularity of the semigroup (
2.4
).

Assumption 2.9
.

For all function
\varphi\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, the map

u:(t,x)\mapsto\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X(t))|X_{0}=x\right]

belongs to
C^{\infty}((0,T),C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}))
, that is, for all
k\geq 0
there exist constants
C>0
and
\kappa\geq 0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{M}

\underset{t\in(0,T)}{\sup}\left|\partial^{k}_{t}u(t,x)\right|\leq C(1+r^{\kappa}_{w}(x))\left|\varphi\right|_{C^{2N+2}}.

Hence, taking the limit as
t\to\infty
of the time integration of Equation (
2.4
) leads to, under
Assumption
2.9
,

\underset{t\to\infty}{\lim}u(t,x)=\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}u(s,x)\mathrm{d}s.

(2.10)

The following assumption is an ergodicity condition concerning convergence to
the invariant measure. We introduce the following norm and seminorm:

\left\|\psi\right\|_{C^{k}}=\underset{\underset{0\leq\left|j\right|\leq k}{j=(j_{1},\dots,j_{D})}}{\sup}\underset{x\in\mathcal{M}}{\sup}\left|\partial_{j}\psi(x)\right|\text{ and }\left|\psi\right|_{C^{k}}=\underset{\underset{1\leq\left|j\right|\leq k}{j=(j_{1},\dots,j_{D})}}{\sup}\underset{x\in\mathcal{M}}{\sup}\left|\partial_{j}\psi(x)\right|.

Assumption 2.10
.

Equation (
1.1
) admits a spectral gap
\lambda>0
: for every integer
k\geq 0
there exists a polynomial
P_{k}
such that for all
t\geq 0
and all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
:

\left\|u(t,\cdot)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq P_{k}(t)e^{-\lambda t}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{k}}.

(2.11)

The Taylor expansion of the semigroup (
2.4
)satisfies the following.

Proposition 2.11
.

Under Assumption
2.9
, for all function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
and
h
small enough, the following expansion holds

u(h,x)=\varphi(x)+\underset{j=1}{\overset{p}{\sum}}\frac{h^{j}}{j!}\mathcal{L}^{j}\varphi(x)+h^{p+1}R^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x),

where the remainder satisfies
\left|R^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x)\right|\leq C(1+r^{\kappa}_{w}(x))
.

Proof.

A Taylor expansion yields:

u(h,x)=\varphi(x)+\underset{j=1}{\overset{p}{\sum}}\frac{h^{j}}{j!}\mathcal{L}^{j}\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{{(h-t)}^{p}}{p!}\partial_{t}^{p+1}u(t,x)\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, the remainder satisfies

\displaystyle\left|R_{p}^{h}(\varphi,x)\right|=\left|\frac{h^{-1}}{p!}\int_{0}^{t}{(\frac{{(h-t)}}{h})}^{p}\partial_{t}^{p+1}u(t,x)\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq\frac{h^{-1}}{p!}\int_{0}^{t}C(1+{r(x)}^{\kappa})\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C}{p!}(1+{r(x)}^{\kappa}),

where we use Assumption
2.9
.
∎

Introduce the Taylor-Talay-Tubaro expansion
[
64
]
of the numerical scheme (
1.4
) and an assumption on its moments.

Assumption 2.12
.

For all function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
and
h
small enough, the expansion holds

\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{1})|X_{0}=x\right]=\varphi(x)+\underset{j=1}{\overset{p}{\sum}}h^{j}A_{j}\varphi(x)+h^{p+1}R^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x),

where
A_{j}
is a linear differential operator of order
2j
and

R^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x)
is a remainder satisfying

\left|R^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x)\right|\leq C(1+r^{\kappa}_{w}(x))\left|\varphi\right|_{C^{2p+2}}.

In the case of a post-processed method (
1.5
), the expansion is of the same form,

\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(\overline{X})|X\right]=\varphi(X)+\underset{j=1}{\overset{p}{\sum}}h^{j}\overline{A_{j}}\varphi(X)+h^{p+1}\overline{R}^{h}_{p}(\varphi,X),

where
\overline{R}^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x)
satisfies

\left|\overline{R}^{h}_{p}(\varphi,x)\right|\leq C(1+r^{\kappa}_{w}(x))\left|\varphi\right|_{C^{2p+2}}.

Remark 2.13
.

For most of the existing numerical methods that have a Taylor-Talay-Tubaro expansion, the differential operators
A_{j}
have a specific form. They typically write with the vector fields
F
and
(E_{d})
and their iterated covariant derivatives, that is, they write with the coordinates of the jet space over
F
and the frame
(E_{d})
.
In the specific case of stochastic Runge-Kutta methods, the
A_{j}
are equivariant with respect to orthogonal change of variables, which results in the
A_{j}
being naturally described by tree structures (see
[
39
,
38
]
). The extension of such universal equivariance property to the frozen-flow methods used here is matter for future work.

Definition 2.14
.

Under Assumption
2.12
, a method is consistent if
A_{1}=\mathcal{L}
. In the following, the consistency of the method is always considered true.

Assumption 2.15
.

The method (
1.4
) has finite moments, that is, for all
\kappa>0
and all
T\in\mathds{R}_{+}
there exists a constant
C(T)>0
such that:

\underset{n=0\dots N}{\sup}{\mathds{E}\left[{r(X_{n})}^{\kappa}|X_{0}=x\right]}\leq C(T).

2.2
High-order characterization of the invariant measure

Let us now state one of the central results of this work. We introduce new sufficient conditions to approximate the invariant measure, adapted from
[
2
]
. This
result is key for designing the high-order numerical schemes (
1.4
) presented in Subsection
2.3
. Proofs of Theorems
2.16
and
2.18
, adapted from
[
24
,
65
]
, are posponed to Section
3
.

Theorem 2.16
.

Consider the SDE (
1.1
) on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying Assumptions
2.4
,
2.8
and
2.10
and solved by an ergodic numerical method (
1.4
) satisfying Assumptions
2.7
and
2.12
and

A_{j}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0\text{ for }j=2\dots p.

Then the method (
1.4
) is of order
p
for the invariant measure for equation (
1.2
). More precisely, the
error of the invariant measure
e(\varphi,h)
satisfies for all
\varphi\in C_{P}^{\infty}
and all
h\in[0,h_{0}]
for a small
h_{0}
:

e(\varphi,h)=h^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(t,x)A_{p+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{O}(h^{p+1}).

Proposition 2.17
.

Under Assumptions
2.4
,
2.9
,
2.12
,
2.15
, and
2.7
, if the Taylor expansion
2.12
satisfies the same expression that in
[
14
]

A_{j}=\frac{1}{j!}\mathcal{L}^{j},\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,\dots,p,

then the integrator is of order
p
for the invariant measure.

This characterization is extended to post-processed method (
1.5
), in the spirit of
[
65
]
.

Theorem 2.18
.

Under the same hypotheses as Theorem
2.16
, consider a numerical consistent ergodic method of the form (
1.5
) that satisfies

A_{j+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=\overline{A_{j}}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0\text{ for }j=1\dots p-1,\hskip 10.00002pt{\left(A_{p+1}+[\mathcal{L},\overline{A_{p}}]\right)}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0.

Then the method is at least of order
p+1
for the invariant measure of equation (
1.2
).

2.3
Post-processed frozen-flow methods of high order for sampling ergodic dynamics

In this section, we leverage Theorem
2.16
to derive
a system of conditions whose resolution yields three new order-2 methods for
accurately sampling the invariant measure. Higher orders can be obtained by
extending the Taylor expansion to the desired degree. Our analysis focuses on
frozen-flow schemes, which generalize classical Lie group
methods
[
34
,
62
]
.
See Section
4
for details of the Butcher series formalism used on the Talay-Tubaro expansion.

Definition 2.19
.

For all
p,q\in\mathcal{M}
,
\exp\left(tg^{d}(q)E_{d}\right)\cdot p
is the solution of the ODE

Y^{\prime}(t)=g^{d}(q)E_{d}(Y(t)),\hskip 10.00002ptY(0)=p.

Remark 2.20
.

The Euler method
X_{n+1}=X_{n}+hf^{d}(X_{n})+\sqrt{2h}\xi^{d}_{n}
on
\mathds{R}^{d}
becomes on
\mathcal{M}
the frozen-flow Euler method:

X_{n+1}=\exp\left(\left(hf^{d}(X_{n})+\sqrt{2h}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}\right)X_{n}.

(2.12)

In general, the method (
2.12
) does not coincide with the Riemannian Langevin method:

X_{n+1}=\exp^{Riem}\left(-h\nabla V(X_{n})+\sqrt{2h}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n},

for equation (
1.2
), based on the Levi-Civita connection and proposed in
[
9
]
.

Following
[
65
]
and the frozen-flow methods
[
14
]
, we consider the following class of methods and we define with the same way the class for the post-processor (
1.5
)

\overline{X}_{N}=\overline{\Psi}_{h}(X_{N}).

\displaystyle H_{n}^{i}=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\left(hZ^{0}_{i,j,K}f^{d}(H_{n}^{j})+\sqrt{h}Z_{i,K}^{d}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}\right)\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\exp\left(\left(hZ^{0}_{i,j,1}f^{d}(H_{n}^{j})+\sqrt{h}Z_{i,1}^{d}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}\right)X_{n},

\displaystyle X_{n+1}=

\displaystyle\exp\left(\left(hz^{0}_{i,K}f^{d}(H_{n}^{i})+\sqrt{h}z^{d}_{K}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}\right)\cdots

(2.13)

\displaystyle\cdots\exp\left(\left(hz^{0}_{i,1}f^{d}(H_{n}^{i})+\sqrt{h}z^{d}_{1}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}\right)X_{n},

where the
\xi^{d}_{n},\overline{\xi^{d}_{N}}
are independent standard Gaussian random variables and where the
Z^{0}_{i,j,k},z^{0}_{i,k},z^{d}_{k},Z^{d}_{i,k},\overline{z_{k}^{d}},\overline{z^{0}_{i,k}},\overline{Z_{i,k}^{d}},\overline{Z^{0}_{i,j,k}}
are fixed real numbers.

Remark 2.21
.

As a forest of ordre
p
contains at most
2p
trees, one only needs to consider approximations of Gaussian random variables with finite moments of all order and the same
2p
first moments that includes vanishing odd moments.

Definition 2.22
.

For a finite subset
S\subset{\{1,\dots,K\}}^{n}
of multi-indices and for
f:S\to\mathds{R}
, the factorial sum is given by

\underset{\mathbf{k}\in S}{{}^{!}\sum}f(\mathbf{k})=\underset{\mathbf{k}\in S}{\sum}\frac{1}{\mathbf{k}!}f(\mathbf{k}),\hskip 10.00002pt(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})!=k_{1}!\cdots k_{n}!.

We now express the general method as an exotic S-serie (see Definition
4.4
), with the coefficient map of the numerical methods
a_{w}
, and the algebraic formalism of Section
4
.

.

2.13

Remark 2.24
.

The use of a post-processor only modifies the order conditions by the coefficient

C_{\left[\mathcal{L},\overline{A_{1}}\right]}=\underset{k}{{\sum}}\overline{z}^{0}_{i,k}-\underset{k_{2}\leq k_{1}}{{{}^{!}\sum}}\overline{z}_{k_{1}}^{d_{1}}\overline{z}_{k_{2}}^{d_{1}}.

If
\overline{A_{1}}\propto\mathcal{L}
, we thus recover the order conditions without post-processor.

Since we only express our conditions in terms of exotic forests, to simplify the notation, we add the coefficients of
\left[\mathcal{L},\overline{A_{1}}\right]
, in the post-processed case, in thoses of
A_{2}
.

Using the Butcher series formalism of Section
4
, we obtain a set of conditions that ensures the order 2 for the invariant measure of equation (
1.2
).

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}).

Proof.

Table
2
gives the reduction of exotic forests of order 2. Therefore, the S-series

A_{2}\varphi=S_{h}(a_{w})\vartriangleright\varphi

can be expressed as a linear combination of irreducible forests, whose coefficients provide the second-order conditions. The details are postponed to Subsection
4.2
. ∎

Remark 2.26
.

Recall from
[
12
]
, for
\mathcal{M}=\mathds{R}^{D}
, the forests are not ordered and there are one condition for the first order and three for the second one.

Remark 2.27
.

For the second order, there are 5 equations with 11 variables and for the third order, there are 40 equations and with 95 variables.
We recall from
[
14
]
that for the weak error, there are 8 equations for the order 2 and 73 for the order 3, so the invariant measure allows for a significant reduction of the number of order conditions.

We propose the following new post-processed frozen-flow method to solve equation (
1.2
). In the Euclidean setting, Method
1
rewrites as the Leimkuhler-Matthews method
[
42
]
with the post-processor formulation from
[
65
]
.

Method 1

Post-processed frozen-flow method for equation (
1.2
)

H_{n}=\exp\left(\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)E_{d}X_{n}
,

X_{n+1}=\exp\left(\frac{5h}{4}F(H_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{4}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{h}{4}F(H_{n})+\frac{3\sqrt{2h}}{4}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n}
,

\overline{X_{N}}=\exp\left(\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\overline{\xi}^{d}_{N}E_{d}\right)X_{N}
.

Corollary 2.28
.

Method
1
is explicit, of order 2 for the invariant measure of equation (
1.2
) and weak order one, and uses one evaluation of
F
and three exponential maps per step.

We propose two alternative schemes without post-processors to solve equation (
1.2
), for the sake of comparison.

Method 2

Stochastic frozen-flow Heun method for equation (
1.2
)

H_{n}=\exp\left(hF(X_{n})+\sqrt{2h}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n}
,

X_{n+1}=\exp\left(\frac{h}{2}F(H_{n})\right)\exp\left(\frac{h}{2}F(X_{n})+\sqrt{2h}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n}
.

Method 3

2-steps frozen-flow Runge-Kutta method for equation (
1.2
)

H_{n}=\exp\left(\frac{h}{4}F(X_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n}
,

X_{n+1}=\exp\left(\frac{h}{6}F(X_{n})+\frac{2h}{3}F(H_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)\exp\left(\frac{h}{6}F(X_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}E_{d}\right)X_{n}
.

Corollary 2.29
.

Methods
2
and
3
are of first order for the weak error and of second order for the invariant measure of equation (
1.2
).

3
High order analysis for the invariant measure

In this subsection, we generalize the analysis of
[
24
]
to Riemannian manifolds and prove Theorem
2.16
and Theorem
2.18
.

Let
{(P_{t})}_{t\geq 0}
be the semigroup associated to the Markov
process
{(X(t,x))}_{t\geq 0,x\in\mathcal{M}}
, in other words,

{(P_{t})}_{t\geq 0}
gives to
\psi
a smooth function on
\mathcal{M}
,
the unique solution
u
of the Kolmogorov equation (
2.4
).
In the spirit of backard analysis
[
24
,
31
]
, let us build a modified generator
\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}
such that the solution of the problem

\partial_{t}v(t,x)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}v,\hskip 10.00002ptv(0,x)=\varphi(x),

at the time
t=\tau
, coincides with the numerical flow, that is

\exp\left(\tau\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}\right)=\id+\underset{n\geq 1}{\sum}\tau^{n}A_{n}.

Thus the expansion yields

\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}=\underset{k=0}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\tau^{k}L_{k}+R_{n},\hskip 10.00002ptR_{n}=\underset{k\geq n+1}{{\sum}}\tau^{k}L_{k}.

Define
\pi_{n}:P\mapsto n!{\left({\partial_{\tau}}^{n}P\right)}_{|\tau=0}
, then

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle=\pi_{n}\left(\exp\left(\tau\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}\right)\right)=\underset{k\geq 0}{\sum}\frac{1}{k!}\pi_{n}\left(\tau^{k}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{k=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\frac{1}{k!}\pi_{n-k}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\tau}}^{k}\right)=\underset{k=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\frac{1}{k!}\pi_{n-k}\left({\left(L_{0}+\tau L_{1}+\cdots+\tau^{n}L_{n}+R_{n}\right)}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{k=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\frac{1}{k!}\pi_{n-k}\left(\underset{j=0}{\overset{nk}{\sum}}\tau^{j}\underset{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=j}{\sum}L_{i_{1}}\cdots L_{i_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{k=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\frac{1}{k!}\underset{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=n-k}{\sum}L_{i_{1}}\cdots L_{i_{k}},

where we use
\pi_{p}(R_{n})=0
when
p\leq n
.
Thus
\left(L_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
satisfies the recurrence relation

L_{0}=A_{1}=\mathcal{L},\hskip 10.00002ptL_{n}=A_{n+1}-\underset{k=2}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\frac{1}{k!}\underset{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=n+1-k}{\sum}L_{i_{1}}\cdots L_{i_{k}}.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\varphi
be in
C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. Under Assumptions
2.10
and
2.12
, there exists a sequence
\left(v_{l}\right)_{l\in\mathds{N}}
, such that for all
n\geq 0
:

\partial_{t}v_{n}(t,x)-\mathcal{L}v_{n}(t,x)=\underset{l=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}L_{l}v_{n-l}(t,x),\hskip 10.00002ptv_{0}(0,x)=\varphi(x),\hskip 10.00002ptv_{n}(0,x)\equiv 0\text{ for }n>0.

Let
v^{N}(t,x)=\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}v_{n}(t,x)
, the we have

\left\|\mathds{E}\left[v^{N}(t,X_{1})\right]-v^{N}(t+\tau,x)\right\|_{\infty}\leq C_{N}\tau^{N+1}\underset{n=0,\dots N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|v_{n}(t_{j}+s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+2}}.

Proof.

For
N=0
, set
v_{0}:(t,x)\mapsto u(t,x)
.
By induction, assume
v_{0},\dots,v_{N}
are defined and denote
F_{N+1}(t,x)=\underset{l=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}L_{l}v_{n+1-l}(t,x)
. Then the Duhamel formula ensures that

v_{N+1}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}F_{N+1}(s,x)\mathrm{d}s.

Assumption
2.10
implies for all
n\geq 0
,
v_{n}
is
smooth and

\left\|v_{n}(t)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq C\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{k+4n}}.

(3.1)

The case
n=0
is ensured by Assumption
2.10
. Suppose that equation (
3.1
) is true for
0,\dots,n
. As
L_{l}
is of order
2+2
, then for all
l=1,\dots,n+1
,

\left\|L_{l}v_{n+1-l}(t)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq C\left\|v_{n+1-l}(t)\right\|_{C^{k+2l+2}}

and by induction
\left\|v_{n+1-l}(t)\right\|_{C^{k+2l+2}}\leq C\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{(k+2l+2)+4(n+1-l)}}
, that is

\left\|F_{n+1}(t)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq C\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{k+4(n+1)}}.

Thus, from Assumption
2.10
, we obtain

\left\|P_{t-s}F_{n+1}(s)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq P_{k}(t-s)e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\left\|F_{n+1}(s)\right\|_{C^{k}},

and

\left\|v_{n+1}(t)\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq C\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{k+4(n+1)}}.

Hence the proof by induction.

Fix
t\geq 0
and set
w_{n}:(s,x)\mapsto v_{n}(t+s,x)
. Then
w_{n}

satisfies

\partial_{s}w_{n}(s,x)-\mathcal{L}w_{n}(s,x)=\underset{l=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}L_{l}w_{n-l}(s,x),\hskip 10.00002ptw_{n}(0,x)=v_{n}(t,x).

Hence
\partial_{s}w_{n}(s,x)=\underset{l=0}{\overset{n}{\sum}}L_{l}w_{n-l}(s,x)
,

\partial_{s}^{2}w_{n}(s,x)=\underset{l=0}{\overset{n}{\sum}}L_{l}\underset{k=0}{\overset{n-l}{\sum}}L_{k}w_{n-l-k}(s,x)=\underset{k=0}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\underset{l_{1}+l_{2}=k}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}L_{l_{2}}w_{n-k}(s,x),

and by induction, it follows

\partial_{s}^{m}w_{n}(s,x)=\underset{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{m+1}=n}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}\dots L_{l_{m}}w_{l_{m+1}}(s,x).

Noticing that
L_{l}
are differentials operators of order
2l+2>0
, the inegality holds

\left\|\partial_{s}^{m}w_{n}(s,x)\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\underset{k=0,\dots,n}{\sup}\left|w_{k}(s)\right|_{C^{2n+2m}}.

A Taylor expansion yields, for the order
2N-n
,

\displaystyle w_{n}(\tau,x)=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{2N-n}{\sum}}\frac{\tau^{m}}{m!}\partial_{s}^{m}w_{n}(0,x)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{{(\tau-s)}^{2N-n}}{(2N-n)!}\partial_{s}^{2N-n+1}w_{n}(s,x)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{2N-n}{\sum}}\frac{\tau^{m}}{m!}\underset{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{m+1}=n}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}\dots L_{l_{m}}w_{l_{m+1}}(0,x)+R_{2N,n}(\tau,x),

where

\left\|R_{2N,n}(\tau)\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\tau^{2N-n+1}\underset{k=0,\dots 2N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|w_{k}(s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+2}}\leq C\tau^{N+1}.

On one hand, we have

\displaystyle v^{N}(t+\tau,x)=

\displaystyle\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\underset{m=0}{\overset{2N-n}{\sum}}\frac{\tau^{m+n}}{m!}\underset{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{m+1}=n}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}\dots L_{l_{m}}w_{l_{m+1}}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{p=0}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{m=0}{\overset{p}{\sum}}\frac{1}{m!}\underset{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{m}=p-m-l_{m+1}}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}\dots L_{l_{m}}w_{l_{m+1}}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{p=0}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{q=0}{\overset{p}{\sum}}\left(\underset{m=0}{\overset{p-q}{\sum}}\frac{1}{m!}\underset{l_{1}+\cdots+l_{m}=p-q-m}{\sum}L_{l_{1}}\dots L_{l_{m}}\right)w_{q}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{p=0}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{q=0}{\overset{p}{\sum}}A_{p-q}w_{q}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x).

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}v^{N}(t+\tau,x)=

\displaystyle\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}\left(\underset{q=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{q}v_{q}(t+\tau,x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\underset{q=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n+q}A_{n}v_{q}(t+\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{q=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\underset{p=0}{\overset{N+q}{\sum}}\tau^{p}A_{p-q}v_{q}(t+\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{p=0}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{p-q\leq N}{\underset{q=0}{\overset{p}{\sum}}}A_{p-q}w_{q}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x).

Thus, the modified flow satisfies

\displaystyle v^{N}(t+\tau,x)-\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}v^{N}(t+\tau,x)=

\displaystyle\underset{p=0}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{p-q>N}{\underset{q=0}{\overset{p}{\sum}}}A_{p-q}w_{q}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{p=N+1}{\overset{2N}{\sum}}\tau^{p}\underset{q=0}{\overset{p-(N+1)}{\sum}}A_{p-q}w_{q}(0,x)+R_{2N}(\tau,x),

and

\displaystyle\left|v^{N}(t+\tau,x)-\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}v^{N}(t+\tau,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\tau^{N+1}\underset{n=0,\dots N}{\sup}\left\|v_{n}(t,x)\right\|_{C^{4N}}

\displaystyle+C\tau^{N+1}\underset{k=0,\dots 2N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|w_{k}(s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+2}}.

Applying Assumption
2.12
with
\varphi=v^{N}(t)
gives

\displaystyle\left\|\mathds{E}\left[v^{N}(t,X_{1})\right]-v^{N}(t+\tau,x)\right\|_{\infty}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\mathds{E}\left[v^{N}(t,X_{1})\right]-\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}v^{N}(t+\tau,x)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\tau^{n}A_{n}v^{N}(t+\tau,x)-v^{N}(t+\tau,x)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}\tau^{N+1}\underset{n=0,\dots N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|v_{n}(t_{j}+s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+2}}.

Hence the result.
∎

We construct a sequence of measure which weakly converges to the invariant
measure of the numerical method (
1.4
).

Lemma 3.2
.

Under Assumptions
2.7
,
2.8
,
2.10
and
2.12
, let
\left(v_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
and
\left(v^{N}\right)_{N\in\mathds{N}}
be defined as in Proposition
3.1
. There exists
\left(\rho_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\infty}
and for all
N\geq 1
:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\rho_{N}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{L}^{*}\rho_{N}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}=\underset{n=1}{\overset{N}{\sum}}L_{n}^{*}\rho_{N-n}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}.

Proof.

Let us show by induction the existence of the sequence
\left(\rho_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
. For
n=0
, set
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\infty}
. Suppose having constructed
\rho_{0},\dots,\rho_{N}
that satisfy these conditions. Then, set
G_{N+1}=\underset{n=1}{\overset{N}{\sum}}L_{n}^{*}\rho_{N-n}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}
. The function
G_{N+1}
is smooth and its integral is

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}G_{N+1}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)=-\underset{n=1}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\rho_{N-n}(x)L_{n}\mathds{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)=0,

as
L_{n}
is a differential operator of order
2n+2
. By
Assumption
2.8
, there exists
\rho_{N+1}\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\mathcal{L}^{*}\rho_{N+1}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}=G_{N+1}
and
\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\rho_{N+1}(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)=0
, which
concludes the induction.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Under Assumptions
2.7
,
2.8
,
2.10
, and
2.12
, let
\left(v_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
and
\left(F_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
be defined as in Proposition
3.1
.
For all
n,k\geq 0
, there exists a polynomial
P_{k,n}
such that

\left\|v_{n}(t,x)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{n}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right\|\leq P_{k,n}(t)e^{-\lambda t}\left\|\varphi-\left\langle\varphi\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k+4n}}.

Proof.

For
n=0
,
v_{0}=u
and
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\infty}
and by Assumption
2.10
, for all integer
k
, we have

\left\|u-\left\langle\varphi\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq P_{k}(t)e^{-\lambda t}\left\|\varphi-\left\langle\varphi\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k}}.

Suppose that the induction is true for
0,\dots,n
. Setting

{c_{n+1}(t)=\underset{m=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)},\hskip 10.00002pt

then
c_{n+1}
is derivable and its derivative is

\displaystyle c_{n+1}^{\prime}(t)=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\partial_{t}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\partial_{t}v_{m}(t,x)\rho_{n+1-m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\underset{l=0}{\overset{m}{\sum}}L_{m-l}v_{l}(t,x)\rho_{n+1-m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{m=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\underset{l=0}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{l}(t,x)L_{m-l}^{*}\rho_{n+1-m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{l=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{l}(t,x)\left(\underset{m=l}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}L_{m-l}^{*}\rho_{n+1-m}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{l=0}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{l}(t,x)\left(\underset{r=0}{\overset{n+1-l}{\sum}}L_{r}^{*}\rho_{n+1-r-l}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x).

Although, by definition of
\rho_{n}
, we have
\underset{r=0}{\overset{n+1-l}{\sum}}L_{r}^{*}\rho_{n+1-r-l}\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}=0
, so
c_{n+1}
is constant and

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\partial_{t}v_{n+1}(t,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)=-\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\partial_{t}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x).

In addition, the computation of the mean
F_{n+1}
with respect to the
measure
\rho_{\infty}
ensures

\displaystyle\left\langle F_{n+1}(t)\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}F_{n+1}(t,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\left(\partial_{t}v_{n+1}(t,x)-\mathcal{L}v_{n+1}(t,x)\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\partial_{t}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x),

by Proposition
3.1
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
. By computing the expression of
v_{n+1}
, we find

\displaystyle v_{n+1}(t,x)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle F_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}\left(F_{n+1}(s,x)\left\langle F_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)+\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(x)\rho_{n+1}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}\left(F_{n+1}(s,x)\left\langle F_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s.

We have

\displaystyle\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}v_{n+1-m}(t,x)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)=

\displaystyle\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\left(v_{n+1-m}(t,x)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{n+1-m}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right)\rho_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x),

as the mean of
\rho_{m}
is zero.
Moreover, the inequality holds

\displaystyle\left\|F_{n+1}(s)-\left\langle F_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k}}\leq

\displaystyle\underset{l=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\left\|L_{l}v_{n+1}(s)-\left\langle L_{l}v_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\underset{l=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\left\|v_{n+1-l}(s)-\left\langle v_{n+1-l}(s)\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k+2l+2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\underset{l=0}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\left\|v_{l}(s)-\left\langle v_{l}(s)\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k+2(n+1-l)+2}},

and, the proof is concluded by

\displaystyle\|v_{n+1}(t,x)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(x)\rho_{n+1}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\|_{C^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underset{m=1}{\overset{n+1}{\sum}}\left\|v_{n+1-m}(t,x)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{n+1-m}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right\|_{C^{k}}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\left|\rho_{m}(x)\right|\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}p_{k}(t-s)e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\left\|F_{n+1}(s)-\left\langle F_{n+1}(s)\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{k}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P_{k,n+1}(t)e_{-\lambda t}\left\|\varphi-\left\langle\varphi\right\rangle\right\|_{C^{4+4(n+1)}},

by induction and Assumption
2.10
.
∎

To be able to prove Theorem
2.16
, we recall
a lemma from
[
2
]
, that is the central result for the
proof of the theorem.

Lemma 3.4
.

Under the same assumptions of Lemma
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
, setting
\rho_{N}^{h}=\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}h^{n}\rho_{n}
, there exists
C_{N}>0
such that

\left|\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{N}^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|\leq C_{N}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}h^{N+1}.

Proof.

We bound the difference between
\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]
and the mean of
\varphi
with respect to the measure
\rho_{N}^{h}
,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]-

\displaystyle v^{N+1}(t_{p},x)=\mathds{E}\left[v^{N+1}(0,X_{p})\right]-v^{N+1}(t_{p},x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\underset{j=0}{\overset{p-1}{\sum}}\mathds{E}\left[v^{N+1}(t_{j},X_{p-j})-v^{N+1}(t_{j+1},X_{p-(j+1)})|X_{p-(j+1)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\underset{j=0}{\overset{p-1}{\sum}}\mathds{E}\left[v^{N+1}(t_{j},X_{1}(X_{p-(j+1)}))-v^{N+1}(t_{j+1},X_{p-(j+1)})|X_{p-(j+1)}\right]\right].

However, we recall Proposition
3.1
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathds{E}\left[v^{N+1}(t_{j},X_{1}(X_{p-(j+1)}))-v^{N+1}(t_{j+1},X_{p-(j+1)})|X_{p-(j+1)}\right]\\
\leq C_{N}\tau^{N+2}\underset{n=0,\dots N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|v_{n}(t_{j}+s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+6}}.\end{array}

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]-v^{N+1}(t_{p},x)\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}\tau^{N+2}\underset{j=0}{\overset{p-1}{\sum}}\underset{n=0,\dots N}{\underset{s\in(0,\tau)}{\sup}}\left\|v_{n}(t_{j}+s,x)\right\|_{C^{4N+6}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}\tau^{N+2}\underset{j=0}{\overset{p-1}{\sum}}Q_{N}(t_{j})e^{-\lambda t_{j}}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}\tau^{N+1}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}.

Since
v^{N+1}(t_{p},x)=v^{N}(t_{p},x)+\tau^{N+1}v_{N+1}(t_{p},x)
and
\left\|v_{N+1}(t_{p},x)\right\|_{C^{0}}\leq C_{N}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{4(N+1)}}
, we find

\left|\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]-v^{N}(t_{p},x)\right|\leq C_{N}\tau^{N+1}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\left|\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{N}^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{p})\right]-v^{N}(t_{p},x)\right|+\left|v^{N}(t_{p},x)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{N}^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N}\tau^{N+1}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}+\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}P_{0,N}(t_{p})e^{-\lambda t_{p}}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{0+4n}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(C_{N}\tau^{N+1}+P_{N}(t_{p})e^{-\lambda t_{p}}\right)\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8N+10}}.

Hence the result by taking the limit in
p
.
∎

With this analysis, we now prove the
Theorem
2.16
.

Proof of Theorem
2.16
.

Under Assumptions
2.8
, we set the sequence
\left(\rho_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
defined in Lemma
3.2
.
The error is defined by

e(\varphi,h)=\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{N+1}\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\varphi(X_{n})-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)

and since
\left(X_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
is ergodic, we have

\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{N+1}\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\varphi(X_{n})=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y).

Our aim is to show that

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle h^{r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(t,x)A_{r+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)+\mathcal{O}(h^{r+1}).

Lemma
3.2
gives a sequence
\left(\rho_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
such that
\rho_{0}=\rho_{\infty}
, and for all
N\geq 1

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\rho_{N}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)=0\text{ and }\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}=\underset{l=1}{\overset{N}{\sum}}L_{l}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N-l}.

Moreover, we have

\left|\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{N}^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|\leq Ch^{N+1}.

For all
n=1,\dots,r-1
,
\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}=A_{n}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=L_{n}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
and
\rho_{n}=0
stands by induction. Thus the inequality
of Lemma
3.4
ensures that

\left|\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{r}^{h}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|\leq C_{r}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8r+10}}h^{r+1}.

However
\rho_{r}^{h}(y)=\rho_{\infty}+h^{r}\rho_{r}
and

\left|e(\varphi,h)-h^{r}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{r}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\right|\leq C_{r}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{C^{8r+10}}h^{r+1}.

The integration of equation (
2.4
), ensures
u(t,x)=\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}u(s,x)\mathrm{d}s
and by ergodicity

\underset{t\to\infty}{\lim}u(t,x)=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y).

So by using that the mean of
\rho_{r}
is zero and that, by ergodicity and equation (
2.10
),

\varphi(x)=\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}u(s,x)\mathrm{d}s,

we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(x)\rho_{r}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}u(s,x)\rho_{r}(x)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(s,x)\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{r}(x)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(s,x)A_{r+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)\mathrm{d}s,

where
\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{r}=A_{r+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}
as in the induction.
∎

We conclude this section by the proof of Theorem
2.18
.

Proof of Theorem
2.18
.

Theorem
2.18
is proven by applying Theorem
2.16
with
\varPhi=\varphi\circ G
where
\varphi
is a test function and
G
is the post-processor. First, we compute the term of error of the composition.

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varPhi(y)\rho_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\rho_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)+\underset{j=1}{\overset{p-1}{\sum}}h^{j}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\overline{A_{j}}\varphi(y)\rho_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle h^{p}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\overline{A_{p}}\varphi(y)\rho_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}(y)+\mathcal{O}(h^{p+1}),

however by Assumption
2.7
, the sum is zero.
Hence by equation (
2.10
), it follows

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\overline{A}_{p}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)=

\displaystyle\left(\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\left(\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(z)\right)\overline{A}_{p}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)\right)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}u(y,t)\mathrm{d}t\right)\overline{A}_{p}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(y,t)\left(-\left[\mathcal{L},\overline{A}_{p}\right]^{*}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(y,t)A_{p+1}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)\mathrm{d}t

where hypotheses
{(A_{p+1}+[\mathcal{L},\overline{A_{p}}])}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
are used. We remark that is it the term of order
p
in the error of Theorem
2.16
. Now, Theorem
2.16
can be applied with
\varPhi=\varphi\circ G
and a computation shows that

\displaystyle\underset{N\to\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{N+1}\underset{n=0}{\overset{N}{\sum}}\varphi(\overline{X}_{n})-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi(y)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(y)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(h^{p+1}).

Thus
\left(\overline{X}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathds{N}}
is of order
p+1
.
∎

4
Intrinsic order conditions for the invariant measure

In this section, we use the algebraic framework of exotic Lie-Butcher series introduced in
[
14
]
to derive the order conditions for sampling the invariant measure. After recalling the notion of planar exotic forests, we present a new operation
\IBP
on forests, derived from the Riemannian integration by parts, to express the adjoint of the operators
A_{n}
and generalise
[
39
,
12
]
.
The approach is successfully applied through Theorem
2.16
and Theorem
2.18
to derive the conditions of Theorem
2.25
. We then discuss the form of the exotic Lie-Butcher series describing the adjoint of the operators
A_{n}
with irreducible forests.

4.1
Planar exotic forests

We recall the structure of planar exotic forests, in the spirit
of
[
14
]
which generalize standard B-series
[
20
,
31
,
21
]
to SDEs on manifolds.

Definition 4.1
.

A decorated tree
(\pi,\alpha)
with the decoration set
D
is a connected oriented graph
\pi=(V,E)
with the vertices
V
and the edges
E\subset V\times V
, in which each node has exactly one outgoing edge except for one node, called the root, that has none. The map
\alpha:V\to D
decorates each vertex. A decorated tree is called planar if for all node
v\in V
, the set
\Pi(v)
of predecessors of
v
is ordered. An ordered, possibly empty, list of planar decorated trees is called a planar decorated forest.

An exotic forest is a planar decorated forest with the decorations
D=\{\bullet\}\cup\mathds{N}
,
\mathds{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
which follows
the following rules: if an integer is used as decoration then it must decorate
exactly two leaves. Two exotic trees
(\pi_{1},\alpha_{1})
and

(\pi_{2},\alpha_{2})
are identical if
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
and if there exists an
application
\psi:D\mapsto D
such that
\psi(\bullet)=\bullet
,

\mathchoice{{\psi\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,\mathds{N}}}{{\psi\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=1.65279pt}}_{\,\mathds{N}}}{{\psi\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=1.15694pt}}_{\,\mathds{N}}}{{\psi\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=1.4251pt}}_{\,\mathds{N}}}
induces an automorphism of
\mathds{N}
and

\alpha_{1}=\psi\circ\alpha_{2}
. Define
V_{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to2.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.22394pt\lower 1.84895pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

as the set of
nodes of
\pi
decorated by
and
V_{L}=\{\{v,w\}\subset V|\alpha_{w}(v)=\alpha_{w}(w)\}
as the set of pairly decorated nodes, that we call lianas.
The set of exotic forests is
EF
, the associated vector space is
\mathcal{EF}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(EF)
.

The order of a forest
\pi\in EF
is defined by

There are 95 forests of order 3.

Definition 4.3
.

Let the map
\mathds{F}^{F}:\mathcal{EF}\to T(\mathcal{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}(\mathcal{M}))
be defined for
\pi\in EF
and
\varphi\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
by

\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi)\vartriangleright\varphi=\underset{v\in V}{\underset{i_{v}=1}{\overset{D}{\sum}}}\left(\underset{\{v,w\}\in V_{L}}{\prod}\delta_{i_{v}=i_{w}}\right)\left(\underset{v\in V_{\hbox to1.92pt{\vbox to1.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.95993pt\lower 2.11296pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.91 4.25 C 0.91 4.76 0.5 5.17 0 5.17 C -0.5 5.17 -0.91 4.76 -0.91 4.25 C -0.91 3.75 -0.5 3.34 0 3.34 C 0.5 3.34 0.91 3.75 0.91 4.25 Z M 0 4.25}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.28}{0.0}{0.0}{0.28}{0.0pt}{3.07289pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{\prod}E_{I_{\Pi(v)}}\left[f^{i_{v}}\right]\right)E_{I_{R}}[\varphi],

where
R
is the set of roots of
\pi
,
\Pi(v)
is the set of
predecessors of
v
, both ordered from right to left,
i_{v}
is the indice
associated to the node
v
then and
E_{I}[g]=E_{i_{p}}[\ldots E_{i_{1}}[g]\ldots]
if the set of indices is
I=(i_{p},\ldots,i_{1})
. If
\{v,w\}
is a liana,
i.e. , they share the same integer as decoration, then the Kronecker symbol
\delta_{i_{v}=i_{w}}
ensures that they share the same indice, i.e. ,
i_{v}=i_{w}
.

From
[
14
]
, the operator
\mathds{F}^{F}:(\mathcal{EF},\cdot)\to(T(\mathcal{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}(\mathcal{M})),\cdot)
is a Hopf algebra morphism. In particular,
\mathds{F}^{F}
satisfies

\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi_{1}\cdot\pi_{2})=\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi_{1})\cdot\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi_{2}).

Definition 4.4
.

Given a one form
a\in EF^{*}
, called a coefficient map, an exotic Lie S-series is the following formal power series in
h
in
T(\mathcal{\mathfrak{X}}_{P}(\mathcal{M}))
,

S_{h}(a)\vartriangleright\varphi=\underset{\pi\in EF}{\sum}h^{\left|\pi\right|}a(\pi)\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi)\vartriangleright\varphi.

In the following, we denote by
a_{w}
the coefficient map of the numerical flow, that is the unique coefficient map
a_{w}:\mathcal{EF}\to\mathds{R}
such that

\mathds{E}\left[\varphi(X_{1})|X_{0}=x\right]=S_{h}(a_{w})\vartriangleright\varphi.

4.2
Integration by parts for the invariant measure

The derivation of the order conditions for the invariant measure is performed using multiple integration by parts of the Taylor-Talay-Tubaro expansion against the invariant measure.
In this context, the use of an orthonormal frame proves crucial to ensure that the operations rewrite with planar exotic forests.

Proposition 4.5
.

For
\varphi,\psi\in C_{P}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, the following integration by parts with respect to the Gibbs measure (
2.9
) holds:

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}E_{i}[\varphi]\psi\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi E_{i}[\psi]\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\psi f^{i}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty},

where
F=f^{j}E_{j}
is given by (
1.3
).

Proof.

We recall the general integration by part’s formula
[
59
,
41
]
, for
X=x^{i}E_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}X[\varphi]\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}=-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\Div(X)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}=-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\left(x^{i}\Div(E_{i})+E_{i}[x^{i}]\right)\mathrm{d}\vol_{\mathcal{M}}.

Then, by applying the formula to the invariant measure with
X=\psi\rho_{\infty}E_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}E_{i}[\varphi]\psi\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\left(\psi\Div(E_{i})+E_{i}[\psi]-\psi E_{i}[V]\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi E_{i}[\psi]\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}\varphi\psi\left(\Div(E_{i})-E_{i}[V]\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}.

Proposition
4.5
holds if
\Div(E_{i})-E_{i}[V]=f^{i}
.
Recall the correction term (
1.3
) that is
f^{i}=-E_{i}[V]-g(\nabla_{E_{d}}E_{d},E_{i})
in the orthonormal basis
\{E_{1},\dots,E_{D}\}
. Hence, Proposition
4.5
holds if
\Div(E_{i})=-g(\nabla_{E_{d}}E_{d},E_{i})
. However, by definition of the operator
\Div(H)=g(\nabla_{E_{d}}H,E_{p})
for all
H\in\mathfrak{X}_{P}(\mathcal{M})
, the Koszul formula
[
59
,
41
]
ensures that

\displaystyle 2g(\nabla_{E_{d}}E_{i},E_{d})=

\displaystyle E_{d}\bigl(g(E_{i},E_{d})\bigr)+E_{i}\bigl(g(E_{d},E_{d})\bigr)-E_{d}\bigl(g(E_{d},E_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle g([E_{d},E_{i}],E_{d})-g([E_{i},E_{d}],E_{d})-g([E_{d},E_{d}],E_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g([E_{d},E_{i}],E_{d}).

Similarly, one finds
2g(\nabla_{E_{d}}E_{d},E_{i})=-2g([E_{d},E_{i}],E_{d})
. Hence the result.
∎

Example 4.6
.

Proposition
4.5
yields for
\varphi=E_{d}[\phi]
and
\psi\equiv 1
.

\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}((E_{d}\cdot E_{d})\vartriangleright\phi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=-\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}f^{d}(E_{d}\vartriangleright\phi)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}.

We thus recover

\mathcal{L}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}={\left(f^{d}E_{d}+(E_{d}\cdot E_{d})\right)}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0.

Remark 4.7
.

In general, Proposition
4.5
does not yield elementary differentials that can be represented as exotic forests. For
\psi=f^{i}
, the terms
E_{i}[f^{i}]
and
f^{i}f^{i}
appear, which cannot be represented by exotic forests.
Such terms are called aromas and yield the larger space of exotic aromatic forests in the Euclidean case
[
40
,
38
,
13
]
. The use of aromas is out of the scope of the present paper.

Let us now identify the exotic forests whose integration by part does not write with on exotic forests.

Definition 4.8
.

The set of irreducible forests
\Irred
is the set of exotic forest whose first tree is not a numbered node.
If
\pi\notin\Irred
and its first root is numbered by
n
, we write
\pi=\hbox to8.31pt{\vbox to8.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.15712pt\lower-1.08423pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

Remark 4.10
.

It is well known that Rota-Baxter algebras
[
8
]
are linked to the representation of integration by parts operations.
The IBP operation does not exactly yield a Rota-Baxter structure thanks to the perturbation

We now derive an explicit expression of the adjoint operator
A_{j}^{*}
with irreducible forests. Since
\IBP
removes only the first numbered root, we iterate
\IBP
until the output is a linear combination of irreducible forests.

Definition 4.12
.

The linear reduction operator
\RED
on
\mathcal{EF}
is given by the following limit of stationary sequence:

\RED=\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}{\left(\id_{\Irredv}+\IBP\right)}^{n}.

Definition 4.14
.

Two exotic forests
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathcal{EF}
are equivalent, written
\pi_{1}\sim\pi_{2}
, if the associated differential operators satisfy

\mathds{F}^{F}{(\pi_{1})}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=\mathds{F}^{F}{(\pi_{2})}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}.

Proposition 4.15
.

For all
\pi\in\mathcal{EF}
,
\pi
and
\RED(\pi)
represent the same adjoint operator, that is

\pi\sim\RED(\pi).

The algebraic characterization of Theorem
2.16
rewrites naturally with planar exotic forests.

Theorem 4.16
.

If the coefficient map
a_{w}
of a numerical method satisfies

\underset{\pi\in EF_{p}}{\sum}a_{w}(\pi)\RED(\pi)=0

then its Talay-Tubaro expansion satisfies
A_{p}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
.

In order to compute the second order condition from Theorem
2.25
, Table
2
gives the reduction of second order exotic forests.

Table 2
:
Reduction of exotic forests of order 2.

Proof of Theorem
2.25
.

By linearity and Proposition
4.15
, the computation of Table
2
reduces the second order of the error for the invariant measure as

\displaystyle\underset{\pi\in EF_{2}}{\sum}a_{w}(\pi)\RED(\pi)=

Hence, by Theorem
4.16
, cancelling each coefficients ensures that
A_{2}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}=0
. Rewriting these conditions gives the second order conditions from Theorem
2.25
.
∎

4.3
Exotic S-series expression of the adjoint operators

Let us distinguish the coefficient maps for the weak error
a_{w}:\mathcal{EF}\to\mathds{R}
and for the invariant measure.
Denote by
\left\langle\cdot|\cdot\right\rangle
the bilinear pairing over
\mathcal{EF}
such that, for
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in EF
,

\left\langle\pi_{1}|\pi_{2}\right\rangle=1\text{ if }\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}\neq 0\text{ and }0\text{ else}.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
A:\Irredv\to\mathcal{EF}
be the operator

A:\pi\mapsto{\underset{\tilde{\pi}\in EF}{\sum}}\left\langle\pi|\RED(\tilde{\pi})\right\rangle\tilde{\pi}=\underset{k\geq 0}{\underset{\tilde{\pi}\in EF}{\sum}}\left\langle\pi|{\IBP}^{k}(\tilde{\pi})\right\rangle\tilde{\pi}.

For the coefficient map of a numerical method
a_{w}
, let us define
a_{\mu_{\infty}}=a_{w}\circ A
.

Remark 4.18
.

The operator
A
is invertible and
A^{-1}=\id-{\IBP}^{*}
.

Proposition 4.19
.

The map
a_{\mu_{\infty}}:\Irredv\to\mathds{R}
is the coefficient map for the invariant measure, that is, the error (
2.7
) for the invariant measure of a method of order p is expressed using a S-series indexed only on irreducible forests of order
p
,

e(\varphi,h)=h^{p}\int_{0}^{\infty}\displaystyle{\int_{\mathcal{M}}}u(t,x){\big(\underset{\left|\pi\right|=p}{\underset{\pi\in\Irred}{\sum}}a_{\mu_{\infty}}(\pi)\mathds{F}^{F}(\pi)\big)}^{*}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}(x)\mathrm{d}t.

Examples 4.20
.

Using Table
2
, the computation of
a_{\mu_{\infty}}
gives for the first and second orders:

It is important to mention that the conditions for the invariant measure are not independent and satisfy relations, analogously to the weak context
[
14
]
. However, the map
a_{\mu_{\infty}}
is not a character for the shuffle product as one has for instance

{a_{\mu_{\infty}}(\hbox to3.24pt{\vbox to3.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower 1.45302pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
