Proposition 4.21
.

The coefficient map
a_{\mu_{\infty}}
is a character for the modified shuffle product,

\tilde{{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}}:(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})\mapsto A^{-1}(A\pi_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}A\pi_{2})=(A\pi_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}A\pi_{2})-{\IBP}^{*}(A\pi_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}A\pi_{2}).

Proof.

Definition
4.17
ensures that for all
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\Irred
,

\displaystyle a_{\mu_{\infty}}(\pi_{1})a_{\mu_{\infty}}(\pi_{2})=

\displaystyle a_{w}(A\pi_{1})a_{w}(A\pi_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{w}(A\pi_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}A\pi_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{\mu_{\infty}}(A^{-1}\left(A\pi_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}A\pi_{2}\right)),

where we used that
a_{w}
is a character for
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-4.8mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}{\mkern-4.5mu}\sqcup}
(see
[
14
]
).
Hence the result.
∎

Theorem 4.22
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.16
(or Theorem
2.18
for post-processed methods) if the coefficient map for the invariant measure of a consistent numerical method satisfies

\mathchoice{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=5.65436pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq k}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=5.65436pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq k}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=6.53268pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq k}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=7.11826pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq k}}}\equiv 0,

then the method is of order
k
for the invariant measure.

Remark 4.23
.

If a method is consistent then
\mathchoice{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=5.65436pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq 1}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=5.65436pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq 1}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=6.53268pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq 1}}}{{a_{\mu_{\infty}}\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=7.11826pt}}_{\,\Irred_{\leq 1}}}\equiv 0
.

5
Numerical experiments

5.1
Ergodicity and long-time sampling on
SO_{p}

Our first experiment focuses on
\mathcal{M}=SO_{p}
the compact Lie group of special orthogonal matrices of size
p\times p
. Let
{\left(A_{d}\right)}_{d=1,\ldots,D}
be an orthonormal basis of its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}_{p}
of skew-symmetric matrices for the metric
g_{x}(A,B)=\Trace\left(A^{T}B\right)
,
A,B\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}
. Let
E_{d}(y)=A_{d}y
be the associated orthonormal frame basis. As the manifold is smooth and compact, the assumptions of Theorem
2.18
are satisfied and our analysis applies.

Remark 5.1
.

On a matrix space with
A_{1},\dots,A_{D}
as a basis of the Lie algebra, the frame is
E_{d}(p)=A_{d}p
, the geodesic flow
\exp
is given by the matrix exponential
\Exp
, and Method
1
becomes the following.

Method 1
Post-processed frozen-flow method for equation (
1.2
) on
SO_{p}

H_{n}=\Exp\left(\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{n}A_{d}\right)X_{n}
,

X_{n+1}=\Exp\left(\left(\frac{5h}{4}f^{d}(H_{n})+\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{4}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)A_{d}\right)\Exp\left(\left(-\frac{h}{4}f^{d}(H_{n})+\frac{3\sqrt{2h}}{4}\xi^{d}_{n}\right)A_{d}\right)X_{n}
,

\overline{X_{N}}=\Exp\left(\frac{\sqrt{2h}}{2}\xi^{d}_{N}A_{d}\right)X_{N}
.

Inspired by the experiments in
[
14
]
, we compare the new methods of high order for the invariant measure to the frozen-flow Euler method (
2.12
) and the so-called SFF2 method from
[
14
]
, which uses two random variables and is of weak order 2.
We consider dynamics with the test function

\varphi(x)=\exp\left(-\frac{g_{x}(x-I_{p},x-I_{p})}{2p}\right).

(5.1)

The reference solution is taken as the mean value of the SFF2 method
[
14
]
with the reference time step
h=2^{-12}
.
We compare the order of convergence in long time for two potentials of the form
V=P(\left\|\cdot-I_{p}\right\|^{2})
where
P
polynomial: a quadratic potential well with
P=10X
and a sextic potential with multiple local minima using the cubic polynomial
P=\frac{X}{2}(X^{2}-9X+24)
.
Starting from the initial condition
X_{0}=-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, we observe in Figure
1
the ergodic behaviour of the dynamic as the trajectories explore a neighbourhood of the identity matrix after an initial transient phase.

Figure 1
:
Trajectory on
SO_{3}
with quadratic potential at time
T=2
and step time
h=10^{-5}
, represented by the three columns on the sphere
\mathds{S}^{2}
(Left). Ergodic convergence computed with
10^{4}
trajectories and
h=10^{-4}
for the quadratic potential and the test function
\varphi
(Right). The method used is Method
2
in both computation.

We observe the error curves for the invariant measure in Figure
2
. We observe that the new methods
1
,
2
and
3
exhibit the expected second-order behavior, with a reduced cost compared to SFF2, which confirms our theoretical results. Moreover, Method
1
rapidly reaches the Monte-Carlo threshold in the quadratic case, hinting that it may have a higher order of convergence for this specific potential.

Figure 2
:
Order of convergence in long time for two potentials: quadratic (left) and sextic (right). The mean is computed at the final time
T=10
with
10^{8}
trajectories and with the test function
\varphi
given by (
5.1
).

5.2
Von-Mises Fisher dynamics on the sphere
\mathds{S}^{2}

Our second experiment focuses on the 2-dimensional sphere
\mathcal{M}=\mathds{S}^{2}
. As our new methods rely on orthonormal bases, we consider the standard coordinates on
\mathds{S}^{2}
minus the poles
(0,0,\pm 1)
:

y_{\theta,\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)\cos(\phi)\\
\cos(\theta)\sin(\phi)\\
\sin(\theta)\end{pmatrix}\in\mathds{S}^{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\theta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\phi\in\left[0,2\pi\right),

with the associated orthonormal frame:

E_{1}(y_{\theta,\phi})=\begin{pmatrix}-\sin(\theta)\cos(\phi)\\
-\sin(\theta)\sin(\phi)\\
\cos(\theta)\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptE_{2}(y_{\theta,\phi})=\begin{pmatrix}-\cos(\theta)\sin(\phi)\\
\cos(\theta)\cos(\phi)\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Using the symmetry
S:(x,y,z)\mapsto(z,y,x)
, we define a second frame on
\mathds{S}^{2}
minus
(\pm 1,0,0)
. For one step of the method, we use the first frame basis if
\left|\sin(\theta)\right|\leq 0.6
, and the second frame else. This ensures a bounded Lipschitz constant for the chosen frame in each case and the assumptions of our analysis are satisfied.
Following the experiments of
[
40
,
9
,
14
]
, we consider the potential
V:(x,y,z)\mapsto-25z
, the associated vector field (containing the Ito correction (
1.3
))

F=-\nabla V-\nabla_{E_{1}}E_{1}-\nabla_{E_{2}}E_{2},

and the test function
\varphi:(x,y,z)\mapsto z^{2}
.
This choice of potential confines the trajectories around the north pole of
\mathds{S}^{2}
(see Figure
3
).
The reference solution is chosen as the mean value of the SFF2 method
[
14
]
with the reference time step
h=\frac{1}{25\times 2^{10}}
.

We compare the new methods of high order for the invariant measure with the methods of
[
14
]
. The error curves for the invariant measure in Figure
3
display the expected orders of convergence. In particular, Methods
2
and
3
exhibit second-order behavior, while Method
1
reaches the Monte-Carlo threshold instantly.

Figure 3
:
Trajectory for the Von-Mises Fisher dynamics on the sphere
\mathds{S}^{2}
at time
T=0.4
and step time
h=4\times 10^{-6}
(Left). Convergence order for the Von-Mises Fisher dynamics on the sphere
\mathds{S}^{2}
at time
T=0.4
with
10^{8}
trajectories and with the test function
\varphi:(x,y,z)\mapsto z^{2}
(Right).

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we designed new numerical schemes of high accuracy tailored for the long time sampling of ergodic stochastic dynamics on Riemannian manifolds. We generalized the Euclidean analysis
[
2
,
24
]
to characterize the intrinsic approximation for the invariant measure on manifolds, and presented new algebraic operations on exotic forests
[
14
]
to compute the order conditions in long time.
The analysis applies naturally to Riemannian Langevin dynamics and the new methods, which generalise in particular the Leimkuhler-Matthews method, show second order of convergence for a reduced cost on numerical experiments.

The present paper opens several avenues for future research.
The new discretisations could naturally be combined with popular sampling techniques, such as perturbations
[
45
,
26
,
1
,
15
]
.
The analysis for the invariant measure, as well as the associated algebraic formalism, will be extended for the design of modified equations of arbitrarily high order for sampling SDEs on manifolds and for the challenging derivation of intrinsic stochastic backward error analysis, generalising
[
39
,
13
]
.
Similar to the Euclidean setting
[
17
]
, one could extend the analysis to general SDEs with multiplicative noise and the creation of higher order methods.
Our approach follows the one of deterministic Lie-group methods and thus relies on a curvature-free connection, unrelated to the natural Levi-Civita connection on Riemannian manifolds. We will extend the frozen-flow methods so that they rely only on Riemannian operations. The study of such new methods is already open in the deterministic setting and relies on the challenging general understanding of the connection algebra
[
4
,
54
,
63
]
.
Finally, the new algebraic formalism of planar exotic series is interesting in itself and could be studied for its universal combinatorial, algebraic, and geometric properties
[
52
,
56
,
29
,
38
,
19
]
, but also for its potential applications in different fields, in the spirit of the use of Butcher series and their extensions in rough paths
[
32
,
44
]
, renormalisation theory
[
16
]
, variational calculus
[
37
,
36
]
, approximation of PDEs
[
5
]
, mathematical physics
[
10
]
, and wave kinetic dynamics
[
25
]
.
These projects will be studied in upcoming works.
